=== BUGGY CODE ===
class Solution:
    def findMaximumElegance(self, items: List[List[int]], k: int) -> int:
        dico=defaultdict(list)
        for profit,category in items:
            dico[category].append(profit)
        categories=[]
        for category in dico:
            categories.append(sorted(dico[category]))
        categories.sort(key=lambda x:x[-1],reverse=True)
        def elegance (distinct):
            res=0
            rest=[]
            for i in range (distinct):
                res+=categories[i][-1]
                for j in range (len(categories[i])-1):
                    rest.append(categories[i][j])
            rest.sort(reverse=True)
            if len(rest)<k-distinct:
                return -1
            return res+sum(rest[:k-distinct])+distinct**2
        l,r=1,min(len(categories)-1,k-1)
        mid=(l+r)//2
        while l<r:
            if elegance(mid+1)>elegance(mid+1) or elegance(mid+1)==-1:
                l=mid+1
            else:
                r=mid
            mid=(l+r)//2
        return max(elegance(mid),elegance(mid+1))[-1]

class Solution:
    def findMaximumElegance(self, items: List[List[int]], k: int) -> int:
        dico=defaultdict(list)
        for profit,category in items:
            dico[category].append(profit)
        categories=[]
        for category in dico:
            categories.append(sorted(dico[category]))
        categories.sort(key=lambda x:x[-1],reverse=True)
        def elegance (distinct):
            res=0
            rest=[]
            for i in range (distinct):
                res+=categories[i][-1]
                for j in range (len(categories[i])-1):
                    rest.append(categories[i][j])
            rest.sort(reverse=True)

class Solution:
    def findMaximumElegance(self, items: List[List[int]], k: int) -> int:
        dico=defaultdict(list)
        for profit,category in items:
            dico[category].append(profit)
        categories=[]
        for category in dico:
            categories.append(sorted(dico[category]))
        categories.sort(key=lambda x:x[-1],reverse=True)
        def elegance (distinct):
            res=0
            rest=[]
            for i in range (distinct):
                res+=categories[i][-1]
                for j in range (len(categories[i])-1):
                    rest.append(categories[i][j])

            if len(rest)<k-distinct:
                return -1

                l=mid+1
            else:
                r=mid
            mid=(l+r)//2

I remember LeetCode 2813 "Maximum Elegance of a K-Length Subsequence" solution:
```
class Solution:
    def findMaximumElegance(self, items: List[List[int]], k: int) -> int:
        items.sort(reverse=True)
        res = 0
        cur = 0
        seen = set()
        dup = []
        for i, (p, c) in enumerate(items):
            if i < k:
                cur += p
                if c in seen:
                    heapq.heappush(dup, p)
                else:
                    seen.add(c)
            else:
                if c in seen:
                    continue
                if not dup:
                    break
                cur += p - heapq.heappop(dup)
                seen.add(c)
            res = max(res, cur + len(seen) * len(seen))
        return res
```

Min duplicate 8. Correct. If duplicate category has profits [10,9,8] and k=2, selected first2 [10,9], heap [9], min duplicate 9. Correct. Popping one reduces count by1; number of heap entries for that category reduces by1. When count becomes1, heap entries for that category should be 0. Thus no stale entries. Great! Let's verify with initial selection where category appears multiple times but not necessarily first k? We select first k by profit. For each selected item, if category already in seen, push profit. This means for each category with count m, the first selected item (highest profit among selected in that category) not pushed, the other m-1 pushed. These are exactly removable items (all but one per category). The min removable profit is min heap. Good.

- Pop min profit from heap. Replace that selected item with this item. Total += p - removed_profit. Add category to seen. Distinct count = len(seen). Update ans.
- We don't need remove from heap? popped.

Let's produce final code:

```python
from typing import List
import heapq

class Solution:
    def findMaximumElegance(self, items: List[List[int]], k: int) -> int:
        items.sort(key=lambda x: -x[0])
        
        total = 0
        seen = set()
        duplicate_profits = []
        
        for profit, category in items[:k]:
            total += profit
            if category in seen:
                heapq.heappush(duplicate_profits, profit)
            else:
                seen.add(category)
        
        ans = total + len(seen) * len(seen)
        
        for profit, category in items[k:]:
            if category in seen:
                continue
            if not duplicate_profits:
                break
            
            total += profit - heapq.heappop(duplicate_profits)
            seen.add(category)
            ans = max(ans, total + len(seen) * len(seen))
        
        return ans
```

Need check if `items.sort(key=lambda x: -x[0])` is stable? Doesn't matter. Could use `items.sort(reverse=True)` but if profits equal, categories descending; no issue. Use key.

Let's think about heap validity more deeply with this simplified algorithm. We push selected duplicate profits only when category already in seen during initial selection. But what about a category that appears first in selected, then later in remaining items? We skip remaining same category. So no push. Good.

What about a category that appears first in selected, then later in selected? We push later selected profits. If we pop one of those later profits, the category still has first selected plus maybe other later selected. Heap entries for that category are the later selected profits not popped. Count of selected items in category = 1 + number of unpopped later selected profits. Thus if count >1, heap entries >0. If count ==1, heap entries ==0. Invariant holds. We don't need know category because we never remove a category from seen; we only add. But what if a category's first selected item is removed? It is never in heap, so never removed. Thus each category's "anchor" (first selected item) remains selected forever. Is that always optimal? Suppose category A has selected profits [100,90,80], heap [80,90]. We pop 80, then 90, leaving 100. If we later want to remove A entirely? To add a new category, we remove a duplicate; if A count becomes1, we cannot remove the anchor without losing distinct category. But maybe optimal solution should remove all items from A and add two new categories, increasing distinct? Wait if A count is1, removing it would decrease distinct by1 and adding a new category keeps distinct same, not increase. To increase distinct by 2, you could remove two items from A (including anchor) and add two new categories: distinct before D (A included), after removing two A items, distinct D-1, after adding two new categories, distinct D+1. That increases distinct by 1? Let's calculate: Start D categories including A with count 3. Remove two items from A: A count1, distinct D (still A). Add one new category: D+1. To add two new categories, need remove two items, but if A count becomes1, cannot remove more without losing A. If remove anchor too, A gone, distinct D-1; add two new categories -> D+1. So distinct increases by 1 relative to original? Actually original D, final D+1, same as removing two duplicates and adding two new categories? Wait if remove two duplicates from A (count3 ->1), distinct remains D. Add two new categories -> D+2. That's possible if A count3, heap has two entries (80,90). We can pop both and add two new categories, final D+2. If we wanted to remove three A items and add three new categories, after removing two, A count1, no heap entries; cannot remove anchor. But if we remove anchor too, A gone, distinct D-1; add three new -> D+2. That's same distinct increase as removing two duplicates and adding two new? Let's compare: Original D, remove two duplicates add two new -> D+2. Remove three A items add three new -> D+2. So removing anchor doesn't increase distinct more; it just uses an extra replacement with no distinct gain? Actually removing anchor loses a category, adding new restores it; net distinct +2? Let's formalize: Start categories set S size D. Remove 3 items from A, A removed from S: size D-1. Add 3 new categories: size D+2. Remove 2 duplicates from A, A remains: size D. Add 2 new categories: size D+2. Same final distinct. Profit? Removing anchor (highest profit in A) would lose more profit than removing duplicates (lower profits), so never better. Thus keeping anchor is optimal. Good.

What about category A selected profits [100,90,80], category B selected [95,85], k=5. Heap [80,85,90]? Build: A100 seen; B95 seen; A90 push90; B85 push85; A80 push80 => heap [80,85,90]. cur = 0

    if i < k:

The omitted top-k item is from a category that may still be represented or not. Standard exchange: If omitted item's category is represented, replace the new category item with omitted item, increasing profit and not decreasing distinct. If omitted item's category is not represented, then both omitted item and new category item are unique representatives; replacing one with the other keeps distinct same and increases profit. Thus there is an optimal selection that includes as many top-k items as possible, i.e., all top-k items except duplicates replaced to add new categories. This supports algorithm.

Let's ensure final code is syntactically correct:

```
from typing import List
import heapq

class Solution:
    def findMaximumElegance(self, items: List[List[int]], k: int) -> int:
        items.sort(key=lambda x: -x[0])

        total = 0
        seen = set()
        duplicate_profits = []

        for profit, category in items[:k]:
            total += profit
            if category in seen:
                heapq.heappush(duplicate_profits, profit)
            else:
                seen.add(category)

        ans = total + len(seen) * len(seen)

        for profit, category in items[k:]:
            if category in seen:
                continue
            if not duplicate_profits:
                break

            total += profit - heapq.heappop(duplicate_profits)
            seen.add(category)
            ans = max(ans, total + len(seen) * len(seen))

        return ans
```

Actually a subsequence is a sequence derived by deleting elements, preserving order. The value depends only on the set of selected items, not their order. Therefore any subset of size k is a valid subsequence (take the selected elements in original order). So sorting is allowed because we can choose any subset. Good.

Let's ensure the standard algorithm is indeed for "Maximum Elegance" not some other problem. Let's search memory: LC 2813 solution exactly:
```
class Solution:
    def findMaximumElegance(self, items: List[List[int]], k: int) -> int:
        items.sort(key=lambda x: -x[0])
        res = 0
        cur = 0
        seen = set()
        dup = []
        for i, (p, c) in enumerate(items):
            if i < k:
                cur += p
                if c in seen:
                    heapq.heappush(dup, p)
                else:
                    seen.add(c)
            else:
                if c in seen:
                    continue
                if not dup:
                    break
                cur += p - heapq.heappop(dup)
                seen.add(c)
            res = max(res, cur + len(seen) ** 2)
        return res
```
Yes. Our code equivalent.

If profits can be negative, total may decrease, but distinct may increase, so ans during initial selection might be higher than after full initial selection? I think yes. So okay. But to be robust, we can update ans during initial selection as in standard loop. That would handle negative profits too. Let's adopt that style to be safe and match known solution. We can write:

```
ans = 0
for i, (profit, category) in enumerate(items):
    if i < k:
        total += profit
        if category in seen:
            heapq.heappush(duplicate_profits, profit)
        else:
            seen.add(category)
    else:
        if category in seen:
            continue
        if not duplicate_profits:
            break
        total += profit - heapq.heappop(duplicate_profits)
        seen.add(category)
    ans = max(ans, total + len(seen) * len(seen))
return ans
```

This updates ans after each selected item and each replacement. This is the standard solution. It handles negative profits (though not needed) and avoids separate initial ans. Let's use this. Let's maybe produce final answer as:

```
from typing import List
import heapq

class Solution:
    def findMaximumElegance(self, items: List[List[int]], k: int) -> int:
        items.sort(key=lambda x: x[0], reverse=True)

        total = 0
        seen = set()
        duplicate_profits = []

        for profit, category in items[:k]:
            total += profit
            if category in seen:
                heapq.heappush(duplicate_profits, profit)
            else:
                seen.add(category)

        ans = total + len(seen) * len(seen)

        for profit, category in items[k:]:
            if category in seen:
                continue
            if not duplicate_profits:
                break

            total += profit - heapq.heappop(duplicate_profits)
            seen.add(category)
            ans = max(ans, total + len(seen) * len(seen))

        return ans
```

remaining [99,2] skip, [1,1] skip, [1,1] skip return302. Good.

Let's maybe think about if there is a scenario where replacing a unique selected item with a new category, and also later replacing another duplicate, could yield higher distinct than replacing only duplicates? Suppose selected has D distinct and some duplicates. To increase distinct by t, you need remove t items from categories that remain represented (duplicates) or remove some unique categories and add more new categories? Let's analyze: Start with D categories. If you remove r unique categories and add s new categories, final distinct = D - r + s. Number of replacements = r + s (since each removed item replaced by new item). To maximize final distinct for a given number of replacements t, you want minimize r and maximize s, with r+s=t. Final distinct = D - r + (t-r) = D + t - 2r. So removing unique categories reduces distinct gain by 2 per unique removed compared to removing duplicates. Thus never beneficial for increasing distinct. Profit also worse. So only remove duplicates. Good.

Let's maybe think about if an optimal solution might remove all items from a category (including anchor) to add two new categories, giving same distinct gain as removing two duplicates from that category? Compare: D -1 + m = D + m -1. Same! Interesting. Removing all m items from a category and adding m new categories yields same distinct increase as removing m-1 duplicates and adding m-1 new categories? Let's calculate: Start D. Same distinct. Profit: removing all m items loses the anchor (highest profit) plus duplicates; removing m-1 duplicates loses only duplicates. So removing all is worse profit. Thus keeping anchor optimal. Good.

</think>

=== RAW ANSWER ===
```python
from typing import List
import heapq

class Solution:
    def findMaximumElegance(self, items: List[List[int]], k: int) -> int:
        items.sort(key=lambda x: x[0], reverse=True)

        total = 0
        seen = set()
        duplicate_profits = []

        for profit, category in items[:k]:
            total += profit
            if category in seen:
                heapq.heappush(duplicate_profits, profit)
            else:
                seen.add(category)

        ans = total + len(seen) * len(seen)

        for profit, category in items[k:]:
            if category in seen:
                continue
            if not duplicate_profits:
                break

            total += profit - heapq.heappop(duplicate_profits)
            seen.add(category)
            ans = max(ans, total + len(seen) * len(seen))

        return ans
```

=== FIXED CODE ===
from typing import List
import heapq

class Solution:
    def findMaximumElegance(self, items: List[List[int]], k: int) -> int:
        items.sort(key=lambda x: x[0], reverse=True)

        total = 0
        seen = set()
        duplicate_profits = []

        for profit, category in items[:k]:
            total += profit
            if category in seen:
                heapq.heappush(duplicate_profits, profit)
            else:
                seen.add(category)

        ans = total + len(seen) * len(seen)

        for profit, category in items[k:]:
            if category in seen:
                continue
            if not duplicate_profits:
                break

            total += profit - heapq.heappop(duplicate_profits)
            seen.add(category)
            ans = max(ans, total + len(seen) * len(seen))

        return ans
